# Nicolás Maduro

"Maduro" redirects here. For other people, see [Maduro (surname)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maduro_\(surname\)).

In this name that follows [Hispanic American naming customs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naming_customs_of_Hispanic_America), the first or paternal [surname](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname) is&#x20;*&#x20;Maduro* and the second or maternal family name is&#x20;*&#x20;Moros*.

**Nicolás Maduro Moros**[^cite_note-16] (born 23 November 1962) is a Venezuelan politician and former [union](17626.md) leader who served as the 53rd [president of Venezuela](3329692.md) from 2013 until his capture during the [United States intervention in Venezuela](82021123.md) in 2026 for alleged [drug trafficking](218449.md) to which he pleaded not guilty. Originally elected democratically following the death of [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez), he shifted toward authoritarian rule, with political analysts eventually classifying his government as a [dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dictatorship) in 2017. Although he was *[de facto](43594.md)* removed from power,[^cite_note-22] according to the [Venezuelan government](78643.md), he is still the *[de jure](43594.md)* president of Venezuela. A member of the [United Socialist Party of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela) (PSUV), he served as the [vice president of Venezuela](23535071.md) from 2012 to 2013 and as [minister of foreign affairs](26105673.md) from 2006 to 2012. Ideologically a [Chavista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavista), his developments to the ideology are called *[Madurismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madurismo)*.

Initially a [bus driver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_driver), Maduro rose to become a [trade union](17626.md) leader before being elected to the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)) in 2000. He was appointed to a number of positions under Chávez,[^cite_note-23] serving as [President of the National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_National_Assembly_of_Venezuela), [Minister of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ministers_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_Venezuela), and vice president under Chávez. Maduro assumed the presidency after [Chávez's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) and won the [2013 special presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election), becoming the 53rd president of Venezuela. He ruled Venezuela [by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree) after 2015 through powers granted to him by the ruling party legislature.[^cite_note-WPdecree-24][^cite_note-25]

Maduro's popularity declined following [shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela) and a drop in living standards which led to [a wave of protests in 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Venezuelan_protests) that escalated into daily marches nationwide and repression of dissent.[^cite_note-CNBCanalysts-26][^cite_note-BInightmare-27][^cite_note-28] An [opposition-led National Assembly was elected in 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election), but Maduro maintained power through the [Supreme Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)), the [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) (CNE) and [the military](32460.md).[^cite_note-CNBCanalysts-26][^cite_note-BInightmare-27][^cite_note-29] The Supreme Tribunal stripped the elected National Assembly of power and authority, resulting in a [constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis) and [another wave of protests in 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_protests). In response to the protests, the [Constituent Assembly of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Constituent_Assembly_of_Venezuela) was elected in 2017 under voting conditions that the opposition alleged were irregular.[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

In 2018, Maduro was [reelected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election) and [sworn in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro). The president of the National Assembly, [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3), was declared interim president by the opposition legislative body, starting [a presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis).[^cite_note-Financial_Times-2019-33][^cite_note-Herrero-2019-34][^cite_note-ZerpaHuyo-35][^cite_note-TSJ-36][^cite_note-pbs.org-37] In 2024, Maduro [was reelected](60825396.md) for a third term, though evidence showed that he lost the election by a wide margin.[^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2024-38][^cite_note-BBC2Aug-39][^cite_note-GuardianEvidenceShows-40]

Maduro was accused of leading an [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism) government, characterized by [electoral fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Venezuela), [human rights abuses](23438021.md), [corruption](23453241.md), [censorship](19533311.md) and [severe economic hardship](52966258.md).[^cite_note-41] The United Nations (UN) and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) have alleged that under Maduro's administration, thousands of people died in [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing) and seven million Venezuelans [fled the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_refugee_crisis) due to economic collapse.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-independent.co.uk2-43][^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46] Maduro has denied all allegations of misconduct and argued that the US has conspired against Venezuela to manufacture a crisis to enact regime change.[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-48]

## Early and personal life



Maduro and his wife, [Cilia Flores](9530834.md), in 2019

Nicolás Maduro Moros was born on 23 November 1962 in [Caracas](55289.md),[^cite_note-fn1-15] into a working-class family.[^cite_note-elmundo2012-49][^cite_note-aljazeera-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-GUARD2013-52] His father, Nicolás Maduro García, was a prominent trade union leader and a "militant dreamer" of the [Movimiento Electoral del Pueblo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movimiento_Electoral_del_Pueblo) (MEP);[^cite_note-minci.gob.ve-53][^cite_note-54] he died in a motor vehicle accident on 22 April 1989. His mother, Teresa de Jesús Moros, was born in [Cúcuta](623811.md), a Colombian city on the border with Venezuela.[^cite_note-Oropeza-2013-55] Maduro was raised in Calle 14, a street in Los Jardines, El Valle, a working-class neighborhood on the western outskirts of Caracas.[^cite_note-Oropeza-2013-55] The only male of four siblings, he has three sisters: María Teresa, Josefina, and Anita.[^cite_note-minci.gob.ve-53]

Maduro was raised [Catholic](606848.md). In 2012, it was reported by *[The New York Times](30680.md)* that he was a follower of Indian [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) guru [Sathya Sai Baba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sathya_Sai_Baba) and previously visited the guru in India in 2005.[^cite_note-New_York_Times2012-12-22-56] In a 2013 interview, Maduro stated that his grandparents were Jewish, from a [Sephardic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sephardic_Jews) background, and converted to Catholicism in Venezuela.[^cite_note-57]

Maduro has been married twice. His first marriage was to Adriana Guerra Angulo, with whom he had his only son, [Nicolás Maduro Guerra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_Guerra), also known as "Nicolasito",[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] who was appointed to several senior government posts, Chief of the Presidency's Special Inspectors Body, head of the National Film School, and is a deputy to the [National Assembly of Venezuela](510768.md).[^cite_note-FOXson-60]

On 15 July 2013, he married [Cilia Flores](9530834.md), a lawyer and politician who replaced Maduro as [president of the National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_National_Assembly_of_Venezuela) in August 2006, when he resigned to become [Minister of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ministers_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_Venezuela), becoming the first woman to serve as president of the National Assembly.[^cite_note-csm-61][^cite_note-62] The two had been in a romantic relationship since the 1990s when Flores was [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez)'s lawyer following the [1992 Venezuelan coup d'état attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Venezuelan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempts)[^cite_note-APnephews-63] and were married months after Maduro became president.[^cite_note-64]

While they have no children together, Maduro has three step-children from his wife's first marriage to Walter Ramón Gavidia: Walter Jacob, Yoswel, and Yosser.[^cite_note-65]

Maduro is a fan of [John Lennon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lennon)'s music and his political activism for peace and against war. Maduro has said that he was inspired by the music and [counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_of_the_1960s) of the 1960s and 70s, mentioning [Robert Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Plant) and [Led Zeppelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Led_Zeppelin).[^cite_note-66]

## Early career

### Education and union work



Maduro as member of the [National Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Constituent_National_Assembly) in 1999

Maduro attended the Liceo José Ávalos [public high school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_school) in El Valle,[^cite_note-aljazeera-50][^cite_note-Lopez-2012-67] where he was introduced to politics as a member of the school's student union.[^cite_note-elmundo2012-49] However, according to school records, Maduro did not graduate.[^cite_note-GUARD2013-52]

For many years, Maduro worked as a bus driver for the [Caracas Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caracas_Metro). He founded an unofficial trade union at the company, which had banned unions at that time. He was also employed as a bodyguard for [José Vicente Rangel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vicente_Rangel) during Rangel's unsuccessful [1983 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Venezuelan_general_election).[^cite_note-GUARD2013-52][^cite_note-68]

At the age of 24, Maduro was living in [Havana](49719.md) after being sent by the [Socialist League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_League_\(Venezuela\)) to attend a one-year course at the *Escuela Nacional de Cuadros Julio Antonio Mella*, a political training center directed by the [Union of Young Communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Communist_League_\(Cuba\)).[^cite_note-Oropeza-2013-55] According to [Carlos Peñaloza Zambrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Pe%C3%B1aloza_Zambrano?action=edit\&redlink=1), during Maduro's time in Cuba he was instructed by [Pedro Miret Prieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Miret_Prieto?action=edit\&redlink=1), a senior member of the [Politburo of the Communist Party of Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politburo_of_the_Communist_Party_of_Cuba) who was close to [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro).[^cite_note-69]

### MBR–200

According to [Carlos Peñaloza Zambrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Pe%C3%B1aloza_Zambrano?action=edit\&redlink=1), Maduro was allegedly tasked by the Castro government to serve as a "[mole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mole_\(espionage\))" working for the [Cuba's Dirección de Inteligencia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direcci%C3%B3n_de_Inteligencia) to approach Hugo Chávez, who was experiencing a burgeoning military career.[^cite_note-70]

In the early 1990s, he joined [MBR-200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutionary_Bolivarian_Movement-200) and campaigned for the release of Chávez when he was jailed for his role in the [1992 Venezuelan coup d'état attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Venezuelan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempts).[^cite_note-GUARD2013-52] In the late 1990s, Maduro was instrumental in founding the [Movement of the Fifth Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fifth_Republic_Movement), which supported Chávez in his run for president in 1998.[^cite_note-Lopez-2012-67]

### National Assembly

Maduro was elected on the MVR ticket to the [Venezuelan Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_Chamber_of_Deputies) in [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election), to the [National Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Constituent_Assembly_of_Venezuela) in 1999, and finally to the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)) in [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election), at all times representing the [Capital District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_District_\(Venezuela\)). He was elected as president of the National Assembly of Venezuela, a role he held from 2005 until 2006.

## Foreign affairs ministry (2006–2012)



Maduro and [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) with U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) at the [Summit of the Americas](366108.md) on 19 April 2009

Maduro was appointed Minister of Foreign Affairs in 2006, and served under Chávez in that position until being appointed Vice President of Venezuela in October 2012, after the [presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Venezuelan_presidential_election). According to *[BBC Mundo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_Mundo)*, during Maduro's tenure as foreign minister, "he was considered a key player in pushing the foreign policy of his country beyond Latin American borders to approach almost any government that rivaled the United States."[^cite_note-NationDivided-71]

Venezuela's foreign policy stances during his term included ending [relations with Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations) in favor of the [People's Republic of China](5405.md),[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] support for [Libya under Muammar Gaddafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Libya_under_Muammar_Gaddafi), breaking off [diplomatic ties with Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations) during the [2008–09 Gaza War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_War_\(2008%E2%80%9309\)),[^cite_note-74] [recognizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_recognition_of_the_State_of_Palestine) and [establishing diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations) with the [State of Palestine](241405.md),[^cite_note-75] a [turnaround in relations with Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombia%E2%80%93Venezuela_relations) in [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Andean_diplomatic_crisis) and again in [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Colombia%E2%80%93Venezuela_diplomatic_crisis),[^cite_note-cnn-76] [recognizing](19042397.md) [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md) as independent states,[^cite_note-77] and support for [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) during the [Syrian Civil War](30741795.md).[^cite_note-78]

Temir Porras, a 2019 visiting professor at [Paris Institute of Political Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sciences_Po) who was Maduro's chief of staff during his tenure as foreign minister, said that in the early days of [Chavismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavismo), Maduro was considered "pragmatic" and a "very skilled politician" who was "good at negotiating and bargaining".[^cite_note-Porras-79] According to [Rory Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rory_Carroll), Maduro did not speak any foreign languages while serving as the Minister of Foreign Affairs.[^cite_note-80]

### 2006 detention in New York

In New York City in September 2006, while attempting to travel back to Venezuela via [Miami](53846.md), Maduro was briefly detained by [United States Department of Homeland Security](58236.md) officers at the [John F. Kennedy International Airport](97218.md) for around 90 minutes, after paying for three airline tickets in cash. Both Maduro and President Hugo Chávez were in New York City attending the 61st session of the [UN General Assembly](31957.md), where President Chávez called U.S. President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) "the devil" during [his speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Ch%C3%A1vez_speech_at_the_United_Nations).[^cite_note-81]

The incident began when Maduro tried to pick up an item that had been screened at a security checkpoint at the airport, and security personnel told Maduro that he was prohibited from doing so. Maduro later identified himself as a diplomat from the Venezuelan government, but officials still escorted him to a room to conduct secondary screening.[^cite_note-82] At one point, authorities ordered Maduro and other Venezuelan officials to spread their arms and legs and be frisked, but Maduro and others forcefully refused. His diplomatic passport and ticket were retained for a time but eventually returned.[^cite_note-NBCN06-83]

Speaking at the Venezuelan mission to the UN after his release, Maduro said his detention by the US authorities was illegal and he filed a complaint at the United Nations. US and UN officials called the incident "regrettable" but said Maduro had been identified for "secondary screening". Homeland Security spokesman Russ Knocke claimed that Maduro was not mistreated, saying that there was no evidence of abnormalities during the screening process. Maduro said the incident prevented him from traveling home on the same day.[^cite_note-NBCN06-83]

When he was informed of the incident, President Chávez said Maduro's detention was retaliation for [his own speech at the UN General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez_speech_at_the_United_Nations) and stated that the authorities detained Maduro over his links [to the Venezuelan failed coup in 1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Venezuelan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempts), a charge that President Chávez denied.[^cite_note-NBCN06-83]

## Vice presidency (2012–2013)

> My firm opinion, as clear as the full moon – irrevocable, absolute, total – is ... that you elect Nicolas Maduro as President. I ask this of you from my heart. He is one of the young leaders with the greatest ability to continue, if I cannot.

— Hugo Chávez during a nationwide TV address ([cadena nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_nacional)) (8 December 2012)

[^cite_note-cnn-76]

Prior to his appointment to the vice presidency, Maduro had been chosen by Chávez in 2011 to succeed him in the presidency if he were to die from cancer. This choice was made due to Maduro's loyalty to Chávez and because of his good relations with other [chavistas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavismo) such as [Elías Jaua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El%C3%ADas_Jaua), former minister [Jesse Chacón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Chac%C3%B3n) and [Jorge Rodríguez](8767262.md). Bolivarian officials predicted that following Chávez's death, Maduro would have political difficulties and that Venezuela would experience instability.[^cite_note-84]

On 13 October 2012, Chávez appointed Maduro Vice President of Venezuela, shortly after Chavez' victory in [that month's presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Venezuelan_presidential_election). On 8 December 2012, Chávez announced that his recurring cancer had returned and that he would be returning to [Cuba](5042481.md) for emergency surgery and further medical treatment. Chávez said that, should his condition worsen and a new presidential election be called to replace him, Venezuelans should vote for Maduro to succeed him. This was the first time that Chávez named a potential successor to his movement, as well as the first time he publicly acknowledged the possibility of his death.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

Chávez's endorsement of Maduro sidelined [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello), a former vice president and powerful [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Socialist_Party_of_Venezuela) official with ties to the [armed forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Armed_Forces_of_the_Bolivarian_Republic_of_Venezuela), who had been widely considered a top candidate to be Chávez's successor. After Maduro was endorsed by Chávez, Cabello "immediately pledged loyalty" to both men.[^cite_note-87]

### Interim president

Upon the death of Hugo Chávez on 5 March 2013, Maduro assumed the powers and responsibilities of the president. He appointed [Jorge Arreaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Arreaza) to take his place as vice president. Since Chávez died within the first four years of his term, the [Constitution of Venezuela](1211684.md) stated that a presidential election had to be held within 30 days of his death.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90] Maduro was unanimously chosen as the Socialist Party's candidate in the election.[^cite_note-91] When he assumed temporary power, opposition leaders argued that Maduro violated articles 229, 231, and 233 of the Venezuelan Constitution, by assuming power over the president of the National Assembly.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93]

## Presidency (2013–2026)

Main article: [Presidency of Nicolás Maduro](51287559.md)



Maduro taking the [oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office) as president of Venezuela on 19 April 2013

The succession to the presidency of Maduro in 2013, according to Corrales and Penfold, was due to multiple mechanisms established by Maduro's predecessor, Chávez. Initially, oil prices were high enough for Maduro to maintain necessary spending for support, specifically with the military. Foreign ties that were established by Chávez were also used by Maduro as he applied skills that he had learned while serving as a foreign minister. Finally, the PSUV and government institutions aligned behind Maduro, and "the regime used the institutions of repression and [autocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocracy), also created under Chávez, to become more repressive [vis-à-vis](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/vis-%C3%A0-vis) the opposition".[^cite_note-94]

In April 2013, Maduro was elected president, narrowly defeating opposition candidate [Henrique Capriles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles) with just 1.5% of the vote separating the two. Capriles demanded a recount, refusing to recognize the outcome as valid.[^cite_note-95] Maduro was inaugurated as president on 19 April, after the election commission had promised a full audit of the election results.[^cite_note-sworn-96][^cite_note-97] In October 2013, he announced the creation of a new agency, the Vice Ministry of Supreme Happiness, to coordinate social programmes.[^cite_note-98]



President Maduro among other Latin American leaders participating in a 2013 [UNASUR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_South_American_Nations) summit

Opposition leaders in Venezuela delivered a May 2016 petition to the [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_of_Venezuela) (CNE) calling for a recall referendum, with the populace to vote on whether to remove Maduro from office.[^cite_note-BBC_June2016_referendum-99] On 5 July 2016, the Venezuelan intelligence service detained five opposition activists involved with the recall referendum, with two other activists of the same party, [Popular Will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_Will), also arrested.[^cite_note-WSJ_Ouster_-100] After delays in verification of the signatures, protestors alleged the government was intentionally delaying the process. The government, in response, argued the protestors were part of a plot to topple Maduro.[^cite_note-101] On 1 August 2016, the CNE announced that enough signatures had been validated for the recall process to continue. While opposition leaders pushed for the recall to be held before the end of 2016, allowing a new presidential election to take place, the government vowed a recall would not occur until 2017, ensuring the current vice president would potentially come to power.[^cite_note-102]

In May 2017, Maduro proposed the [2017 Venezuelan Constituent Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_Constituent_Assembly_election), which was later held on 30 July 2017 despite wide international condemnation.[^cite_note-LaLista-103][^cite_note-WPeuroMEX-104] The United States sanctioned Maduro following the election, labeling him as a "dictator", preventing him from entering the United States.[^cite_note-SANCTIONED-105] Other nations, such as China,[^cite_note-106] Russia,[^cite_note-107] and Cuba[^cite_note-108] offered their support to Maduro and the Constituent Assembly elections. The presidential elections, whose original electoral date was scheduled for December 2018, was subsequently pulled ahead to 22 April before being pushed back to 20 May.[^cite_note-BBCweighs-109][^cite_note-110][^cite_note-VOAdelay-111] Analysts described the poll as a [show election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_election),[^cite_note-ShamAC-112][^cite_note-ShamFT-113] with the elections having the lowest [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout) in the country's democratic era.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

Beginning six months after being elected, Maduro was given the power to [rule by decree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_by_decree) by the pre-2015 Venezuelan legislature (from 19 November 2013 to 19 November 2014, 15 March 2015 to 31 December 2015)[^cite_note-WPdecree-24] and later by the Supreme Tribunal (since 15 January 2016) to address [the ongoing economic crisis](52966258.md) in Venezuela, with strong condemnation by the Venezuelan opposition claiming that the legislature's power had been usurped by the court.[^cite_note-DECREE8-116][^cite_note-117] His presidency has coincided with a decline in Venezuela's socioeconomic status, with crime, inflation, poverty and hunger increasing; analysts have attributed Venezuela's decline to both Chávez and Maduro's economic policies,[^cite_note-118][^cite_note-ELPAISfeb2015-119] while Maduro has blamed [speculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speculative_attack) and [economic warfare](4757142.md) waged by his political opponents.[^cite_note-120]

A 2018 [Amnesty International](18947898.md) report "accused Nicolas Maduro's government of committing some of the worst human rights violations in Venezuela's history".[^cite_note-VOA_news-2018-121] The report found the violence was carried out especially in Venezuela's poor neighborhoods, and included "8,292 extrajudicial executions carried out between 2015 and 2017".[^cite_note-VOA_news-2018-121] In one year, 22% of homicides (4,667) were committed by security forces.[^cite_note-VOA_news-2018-121] Amnesty International's [Erika Guevara-Rosas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erika_Guevara_Rosas) said, "The government of President Maduro should guarantee the right to life, instead of taking the lives of the country's young people."[^cite_note-VOA_news-2018-121]



Maduro meeting with U.S. Secretary of State [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) on 26 September 2016

During the later years of Maduro's presidency, pro-government police and military forces launched the "[Liberation of the People Operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_of_the_People_Operation?action=edit\&redlink=1)", which they stated targeted [street gangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_gangs) and non-state paramilitary formations which they alleged had taken control of poor neighbourhoods. The operations reportedly resulted in thousands of arrests and an estimated 9,000 deaths, with the Venezuelan opposition claiming that the operations are actually a state instrument of repression. The UN released a report condemning the violent methods of the operation. Although the Venezuelan government's [ombudsman](26289198.md), [Tarek William Saab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarek_William_Saab) has admitted that his office received dozens of reports of "police excesses", he defended the need for the operations and stated that his office would be working alongside the police and military "to safeguard human rights". The Venezuelan Foreign Ministry has criticised the UN's report, calling it "neither objective, nor impartial" and listed what it believed were a total of 60 errors in the report.[^cite_note-independent.co.uk-122][^cite_note-123]

In 2019, Porras, Maduro's former chief of staff, said that Maduro "delivered practically nothing in terms of public policy, in terms of direction" during his first term because, in Porras' opinion, "he does not have a clear vision for the country. He is very much focused on consolidating his power among his own peers in Chavismo and much less on exercising or implementing a strategic vision for the country."[^cite_note-Porras-79] Following increased [international sanctions during the Venezuelan crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Venezuelan_crisis) in 2019, the Maduro government abandoned socialist policies established by Chávez, such as price and currency controls, which resulted in the country seeing a rebound from economic decline.[^cite_note-The_Economist-2019-124] *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* wrote that Venezuela had also obtained "extra money from selling gold (both from illegal mines and from its reserves) and narcotics".[^cite_note-The_Economist-2019-124]

On 3 May 2020, Venezuelan security forces [prevented an attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gideon_\(2020\)) to overthrow Maduro by armed Venezuelan dissidents. The attempt was organised by an American private security company, [Silvercorp USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Goudreau#Silvercorp_USA), headed by [Jordan Goudreau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordan_Goudreau) and the men were trained in Colombia. Goudreau claimed the operation had involved 60 troops, including two former US special forces members.[^cite_note-ind040520-125][^cite_note-guardian040520-126] The Venezuelan government claimed the United States and its [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md) (DEA) were responsible for the operation and had support from Colombia.[^cite_note-127] Juan Guaidó denied involvement in the operation. Goudreau claimed that Guaidó and two political advisers had signed a contract with him for US\$213 million in October 2019.[^cite_note-guardian040520-126] Eight of the attackers were killed, and thirteen more, including two Americans, were captured.[^cite_note-Gideon-128][^cite_note-129]



Foreign leaders greet Maduro at [Maduro's second inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) on 10 January 2019

In October 2020, Maduro was indicted by a [US federal court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_federal_court), accused of [narcoterrorism](221350.md) and conspiracy to import [cocaine](7701.md) to the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-guardian20250907-130] In August 2025, the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) raised the reward for the arrest of Maduro to US\$50 million.[^cite_note-guardian20250907-130] The U.S. began a series of escalating actions including the 10 December 2025 [seizure of the oil tanker *Skipper*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_naval_quarantine_during_Operation_Southern_Spear) in international waters, off the Venezuelan coast.

In October 2025, it was reported that the United States allegedly attempted to capture Maduro through a [covert operation](613453.md) involving an effort to recruit his personal pilot, General Bitner Villegas. According to the [Associated Press](18935732.md), U.S. Homeland Security agent Edwin Lopez met Villegas in the [Dominican Republic](8060.md) and offered him wealth in exchange for diverting Maduro's aircraft to a location where U.S. authorities could legalize his arrest, who faced narco-terrorism charges. Over the following 16 months, Lopez maintained encrypted contact with the pilot, but the plan failed when Villegas refused to cooperate. The plot originated from an April 2024 tip about Maduro's planes, which were later seized in [Santo Domingo](57638.md) for sanctions violations. In 2025, after Lopez's final attempts to persuade Villegas collapsed, allies of the Venezuelan opposition publicized the agent's contact with the pilot, briefly sparking speculation about Villegas's fate before he reappeared publicly affirming his loyalty to Maduro.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132]

On 24 November 2025, the [Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration) officially labeled him and his government allies as members of a foreign terrorist group.[^cite_note-Atwood-133]

On 3 January 2026, during the [United States strikes in Venezuela](82021123.md), [Donald Trump](4848272.md) said Maduro [had been captured](82021123.md#Capture_of_Maduro) by the United States, along with his wife.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] Legal experts believe it is likely a violation of the [U.N. Charter](88805.md)'s article 2(4) which requires respect for other countries' sovereignty and prohibits the use of military force without U.N. authorization, except for self-defense.[^cite_note-136] Although Maduro was *[de facto](43594.md)* removed from power,[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] according to the [Venezuelan government](78643.md) and interim president [Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md), he is still the *[de jure](43594.md)* president of Venezuela.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2026-4][^cite_note-Maduro_is_Venezuela-2026-5][^cite_note-Guardian_still_president-6]

### International relations

President Rouhani in meeting with Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro 02

Maduro meeting with [Iranian](14653.md) president [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) in 2015

On 6 March 2014, marking the [1 year anniversary of Hugo Chávez's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez), Maduro announced on [live television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_nacional) that he was [breaking diplomatic and commercial relations with Panama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Panama%E2%80%93Venezuela_diplomatic_crisis) after the country's president [Ricardo Martinelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Martinelli) expressed support for the demonstrators during the [protests that began on 12 February](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Venezuelan_protests) and called on the OAS to investigate.[^cite_note-142] Relations were later restored in July 2014, after Vice President [Jorge Arreaza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Arreaza) attended the inauguration of President [Juan Carlos Varela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_Varela).[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144]

On 11 August 2017, US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) said that he is "not going to rule out a military option" to confront the government of Maduro.[^cite_note-145] On 23 January 2019, Maduro announced that Venezuela was breaking ties with the United States following Trump's announcement of recognizing [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3), the Venezuelan opposition leader, as the interim President of Venezuela.[^cite_note-146]

Another [diplomatic crisis with Panama occurred in 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Panama%E2%80%93Venezuela_diplomatic_crisis), after the Panamanian government imposed sanctions on Maduro and several key officials of the Bolivarian government. Venezuela responded by imposing reciprocal sanctions on Panamanian companies and as well as prominent Panamanian officials, including President [Juan Carlos Varela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Carlos_Varela).[^cite_note-147] The diplomatic crisis ended on 26 April 2018 when President Maduro announced that he had called President Varela and agreed to the return of the ambassadors and the return of air communication between both countries.[^cite_note-148]

On 14 January 2019, days after [Brazil](3383.md) recognised Venezuelan opposition leader Juan Guaidó as the country's interim president, Maduro called Brazilian President [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) "a [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler) of the modern era".[^cite_note-149]



Maduro with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) during the [Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Moscow_Victory_Day_Parade) celebrations in Moscow on 7 May 2025

Maduro has a strategic partnership with Russian leader Vladimir Putin. After [Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022](79400268.md), Maduro discussed increasing cooperation with Russia.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-Taylor-2022-151] Due to the raised oil prices resulting from the conflict, diplomatic conversations were initiated between Maduro and US officials, suggesting the possibility of easing of US sanctions on Venezuela and improved relations between the two countries.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154] Late in the [Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration), the U.S. Department of the Treasury's Office of Foreign Assets Control ([OFAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OFAC)) lifted sanctions in October 2023 for six-months to allow limited trade with the U.S. to resume.[^cite_note-155]

During the [2022 United Nations Climate Change Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference) in November, multiple world leaders interacted with Maduro, including [President of France](24899.md) [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md), [Prime Minister of Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Portugal) [António Costa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_Costa) and American [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry), with President Macron addressing Maduro as "president" and stating "I would be happy if we could talk to each other for longer to engage in useful bilateral work for the region."[^cite_note-Taylor-2022-151][^cite_note-156] Days later on 27 November, the United States eased sanctions on Venezuela and allowed [Chevron Corporation](284749.md) to temporarily work with the Venezuelan government.[^cite_note-157]

Maduro made an official state visit to [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) in June 2023.[^cite_note-158] He also visited [China](5405.md) in September 2023, requesting China's support for Venezuela to join the economic bloc [BRICS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRICS) and saying he wanted China's investment in Latin America and the Caribbean to increase. Brazil's president [Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lula_da_Silva) supported Maduro's request to join BRICS.[^cite_note-159] During the visit, Maduro also signed an agreement that included the training of Venezuelan astronauts, expressing his wish to send Venezuelans to the [Moon](19331.md).[^cite_note-160]



Maduro at the [2023 South American summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_South_American_summit)

In the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md), Maduro has frequently supported the Palestinian cause in international forums, declaring that "[Jesus Christ](1095706.md) was a young Palestinian unjustly crucified by the [Spanish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire)".[^cite_note-161] On 7 November 2023, he condemned [Israel](9282173.md)'s actions in the [Gaza Strip](12047.md) during the [Gaza war](74998519.md) and accused Israel of committing [genocide against Palestinians](75668427.md) in Gaza.[^cite_note-162]

Maduro promoted a [consultative referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Venezuelan_referendum) in Venezuela to support Venezuela's [claim to the Essequibo region](6896458.md), which is disputed with, and controlled by, neighboring [Guyana](17238691.md). The referendum took place on 3 December 2023, and a large majority (nearly 100%) voted in favour of Venezuela's claims, though it had a low turnout.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] The referendum was one of the contributing factors for the [Guyana–Venezuela crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guyana%E2%80%93Venezuela_crisis_\(2023%E2%80%932024\)).[^cite_note-165]

In June 2025, Maduro condemned [Israeli attacks on Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/June_2025_Israeli_strikes_on_Iran), describing it as a "criminal assault" that "violates international law and the United Nations Charter", and accused France, Germany, Britain and the US of supporting "the 21st-century [Hitler](2731583.md)" against the "noble and peaceful Iranian people".[^cite_note-166]

### Assassination attempts

Main article: [2018 Caracas drone attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_drone_attack)

On 4 August 2018, while giving a speech while commemorating the 81st anniversary of the [Bolivarian National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarian_National_Guard_of_Venezuela) and addressing soldiers in front of the [Centro Simón Bolívar Towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar_Towers) and [Palacio de Justicia de Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palacio_de_Justicia_de_Caracas), two drones [detonated explosives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Caracas_drone_attack) near [Avenida Bolívar, Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenida_Bol%C3%ADvar,_Caracas). The Venezuelan government claims the event was a targeted attempt to assassinate Maduro, accusing [Venezuelan National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_National_Assembly) deputy [Juan Requesens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Requesens) of being the attempted assassin.[^cite_note-7del-167] However, the cause and intention of the explosions is debated.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] Others have suggested the incident was a [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) operation designed by the government to justify repression of opposition in Venezuela.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

In May 2020, a failed dissident maritime infiltration known as [Operation Gideon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Gideon_\(2020\)) attempted to enter Venezuela by sea from eastern Colombia. Organized by Jordan Goudreau, a former U.S. Green Beret and CEO of the private security firm Silvercorp USA, the operation aimed to take control of tactical airfields, overthrow the government, and forcefully capture or eliminate Maduro.[^cite_note-GideonOverview-173] The infiltration boats were intercepted by Venezuelan intelligence forces at Macuto and Chuao, resulting in at least six deaths and the apprehension of over 90 individuals, including two U.S. citizens and former Special Forces soldiers, Airan Berry and Luke Denman.[^cite_note-174] Maduro subsequently accused the governments of Colombia and the United States of orchestrating and financing the raid as a targeted terrorist operation.[^cite_note-GideonOverview-173]

In September 2024, the Venezuelan police captured and arrested three Americans, two Spaniards, and a Czech national carrying sniper rifles and other munitions to allegedly assassinate Maduro.[^cite_note-175] In a public statement, the [Minister of Interior, Justice and Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior,_Justice_and_Peace_\(Venezuela\)), [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello) held the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) and [Spanish intelligence](610068.md) responsible for the coup attempt, calling it 'not surprising'. The minister shared that they had arrested yet another US military active-duty member, Wilber Joseph Castañeda, with evidence from his cell phone linking him to terrorist attacks during the 2024 Venezuelan presidential election.[^cite_note-176]

In late 2025, public debate regarding covert U.S. intervention intensified following reporting on historical executive parameters and contemporary intelligence directives. National security analysts and declassified documents published by the National Security Archive highlighted a renewed legal focus on the region, noting that while public executive orders have historically banned targeted foreign assassinations, evolving designations regarding international narco-terrorism networks had fueled widespread speculation from the Maduro administration regarding authorized CIA clandestine operations and covert action strategies.[^cite_note-177]

## United States capture and trial (2026–present)

Main articles: [2026 United States intervention in Venezuela](82021123.md) and [Prosecution of Nicolás Maduro and Cilia Flores](82024138.md)



Maduro following his capture, on board [USS *Iwo Jima*](583918.md)

In the pre-dawn hours of 3 January 2026, the United States launched a military operation in Venezuela.[^cite_note-178] A large-scale strike was reportedly carried out, with [Donald Trump](4848272.md), the [president of the United States](24113.md), announcing around 04\:20 [EST](496028.md) (05\:20 [VET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_Venezuela)) that Maduro and his wife were captured and had been flown out of the country; [Secretary of State](32293.md) [Marco Rubio](5502549.md) said that Maduro was to stand trial on criminal charges in the United States.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2026-179][^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181][^cite_note-182] Senator [Mike Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Lee) stated that Rubio "anticipate\[d] no further action in Venezuela now that Maduro is in U.S. custody".[^cite_note-183] The apprehension was carried out by the US Army's [Delta Force](328809.md).[^cite_note-BBC_News-2026-179] Venezuelan [vice president](23535071.md) [Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md) said she did not know Maduro's whereabouts and demanded "proof of life".[^cite_note-184]

The couple was initially transferred to [USS *Iwo Jima*](583918.md) and subsequently flown to the United States, arriving at [Stewart Air National Guard Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stewart_Air_National_Guard_Base) in [Upstate New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upstate_New_York) on the same day.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] Maduro is being held at the [Metropolitan Detention Center, Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Detention_Center,_Brooklyn).[^cite_note-187]

Maduro was taken into federal custody to face the 2020 narco-terrorism charges in the [Southern District of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_District_of_New_York), with an initial court appearance scheduled for the following week.[^cite_note-188] U.S. attorney general [Pam Bondi](4550623.md) confirmed that both Maduro and his wife would face charges including narco-terrorism conspiracy and cocaine importation conspiracy in a [superseding indictment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superseding_indictment).[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

On 5 January 2026, [Maduro and his wife were both arraigned](82024138.md#First_court_appearance_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_and_Cilia_Flores), with each entering not guilty pleas to numerous drug trafficking charges.[^cite_note-arraignmentandsomeoutcomes-191] During the hearing, U.S. District Judge [Alvin Hellerstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alvin_Hellerstein) ordered Maduro to remain held until at least a March 17 hearing.[^cite_note-arraignmentandsomeoutcomes-191][^cite_note-192] This court appearance was delayed to 26 March 2026. He remained in solitary confinement to protect him from possible attacks by Venezuelan inmates.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] [Maduro and his wife appeared in court on March 26](82024138.md#Second_court_appearance_of_Maduro_and_Flores), after which they returned to jail in Brooklyn.[^cite_note-195]

Their trial has now been scheduled for 1 June 2027.[^cite_note-196]

## Controversies

Further information: [Protests against Nicolás Maduro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro)

### Disputed presidency

Main article: [Venezuelan presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis)



Maduro in January 2019 at the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) building

With widespread condemnation,[^cite_note-Financial_Times-2019-33][^cite_note-Herrero-2019-34][^cite_note-ZerpaHuyo-35] President Maduro [was sworn in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) on 10 January 2019. Minutes afterwards, the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS) approved a resolution declaring his presidency illegitimate and calling for new elections.[^cite_note-197] The National Assembly invoked a [state of emergency](298608.md),[^cite_note-198] and some nations removed their embassies from Venezuela,[^cite_note-Fourteen-199][^cite_note-200] with Colombia,[^cite_note-201] and the US[^cite_note-202] saying Maduro was converting Venezuela into a dictatorship. The president of the National Assembly, [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3), was declared interim president on 23 January 2019;[^cite_note-203] the US, Canada, Brazil and several Latin American [countries supported Guaidó as interim president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Responses_to_the_2019_Venezuelan_presidential_crisis#RecognitionMG) the same day; Russia, China, and Cuba supported Maduro.[^cite_note-TSJ-36][^cite_note-pbs.org-37] The Supreme Tribunal rejected the National Assembly decisions,[^cite_note-TSJ-36] while the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile) welcomed Guaidó as interim president.[^cite_note-204] The [United States Department of State](31975.md) stated that Maduro had used unconstitutional means and a "sham electoral system" to maintain an unlawful presidency not recognized by Venezuela's neighbors.[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205]

Maduro disputed Guaidó's claim and broke off diplomatic ties with several nations who recognized Guaidó's claim.[^cite_note-206] Maduro's government stated that the crisis was a coup led by the US to topple him and control [the country's oil reserves](19100139.md)."[^cite_note-presidencia.gob.ve-207][^cite_note-Europa_Press-208]

### Dictatorship accusations



Maduro speaking at the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) in February 2017

Maduro has been accused of authoritarian leadership since he took office in 2013.[^cite_note-DITboliburg-209] After the opposition won the [2015 parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election),[^cite_note-NYTmuzzle-210] the [lame duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lame_duck_\(politics\)) National Assembly—consisting of [pro-Maduro Bolivarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolivarianism) officials—filled the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) with Maduro allies;[^cite_note-211] the *New York Times* reported that Venezuela was "moving closer to one-man rule".[^cite_note-NYTmuzzle-210]

In 2016, the Supreme Tribunal refused to acknowledge the democratically elected National Assembly's attempts to recall Maduro, and the words *dictator* and *authoritarianism* began to appear: *[Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Affairs)* wrote of a "full-on dictatorship",[^cite_note-212] [Javier Corrales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_Corrales) wrote in *[Americas Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas_Quarterly)* that Venezuela was "transition\[ing] to a full dictatorship",[^cite_note-213] and OAS General Secretary [Luis Almagro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Almagro) said Maduro was becoming a dictator.[^cite_note-214] After election officials aligned with the government blocked an attempt to summon a recall referendum against Maduro, Venezuelan political analysts cited in *The Guardian* warned of authoritarianism and a dictatorship.[^cite_note-215]

The Supreme Tribunal took over the legislative powers of the National Assembly in March, provoking the [2017 Venezuelan constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_constitutional_crisis).[^cite_note-216] With the [2017 Constituent National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Constituent_National_Assembly) poised to declare itself the governing body of Venezuela,[^cite_note-217] the [United States Department of the Treasury](53667.md) sanctioned President Maduro, labeled him a dictator, and prevented him from entering the US.[^cite_note-SANCTIONED-105] Chilean president [Sebastián Piñera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebasti%C3%A1n_Pi%C3%B1era) labeled Maduro a dictator.[^cite_note-218] [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) described the process that led to the National Assembly being taken over, and Venezuela a dictatorship, and said the "Venezuelan government is tightening its stranglehold on the country's basic institutions of democracy at a terrifying speed."[^cite_note-219] The *Financial Times* discussed "international censure of Nicolás Maduro, Venezuela's thuggish president".[^cite_note-220] The *[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune)* wrote that "the Trump administration should harbor no illusions about Maduro, who appears bent on assuming the mantle of dictator."[^cite_note-221] [Vox Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox_\(website\)) published an opinion titled "How Venezuela went from a rich democracy to a dictatorship on the brink of collapse."[^cite_note-222]

The [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) stated that during Maduro's presidency, the country's democracy deteriorated further, with the 2017 report downgrading Venezuela from a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime) to an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism), the lowest category, with an index of 3.87 (the second lowest in Latin America, along with Cuba), reflecting "Venezuela's continued slide towards dictatorship" as the government has side-lined the opposition-dominated National Assembly, jailed or disenfranchised leading opposition politicians and violently suppressed opposition protests.[^cite_note-223]

[Venezuelan presidential elections were held prematurely in May 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election); the *New York Times* printed a piece headlining the word *dictator*, "Critics Say He Can't Beat a Dictator. This Venezuelan Thinks He Can".[^cite_note-224] Miguel Angel Latouche, a political science professor at [Central University of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_University_of_Venezuela) wrote a piece entitled, "Venezuela is now a dictatorship",[^cite_note-225] and *CNN* reported that US Republicans were using the term *Venezuelan dictator* to describe a Democratic candidate.[^cite_note-226] [Roger Noriega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Noriega) wrote in the *Miami Herald* that a "lawless regime" and "narcodictatorship" headed by Maduro, [Tareck El Aissami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tareck_El_Aissami) and [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello) had driven "Venezuela to the brink of collapse".[^cite_note-227]

The 10 January 2019 [second inauguration of Nicolás Maduro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) was widely condemned[^cite_note-SwearsIllegitimate-228][^cite_note-HowDid-229] and led to commentary that Maduro had consolidated power and become a dictator,[^cite_note-230] from the *[Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times)*,[^cite_note-231] the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations),[^cite_note-232] German newspaper *[Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung](513356.md)*,[^cite_note-233] and the *[Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*.[^cite_note-234]

Canada's prime minister [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau) labeled Maduro an "illegitimate dictator" responsible for "terrible oppression" and the humanitarian crisis.[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] Presidents [Mauricio Macri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauricio_Macri) of Argentina and [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) of Brazil condemned what they called Maduro's dictatorship.[^cite_note-238]

Univisión announcer [Jorge Ramos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Ramos_\(news_anchor\)) described his detention following a live interview of Maduro, saying that if Maduro does not release the seized video of the interview, "he is behaving exactly like a dictator".[^cite_note-239] U.S. Senator [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) stated "of course Maduro is a dictator".[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] Reporter Kenneth Rapoza wrote an opinion piece for *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* with the title, "Basically everyone now knows Venezuela is a dictatorship."[^cite_note-242]

### Birthplace and nationality

> To be elected as President of the Republic it is required to be Venezuelan by birth, to not hold another nationality, to be over thirty years old, to not be a member of the clergy, to not be subject to a court conviction and to meet the other requirements established in this Constitution

— Article 227 of the Constitution of Venezuela

Maduro's birthplace and nationality have been questioned,[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] with some placing doubt he can legally hold the presidency, given that Article 227 of the constitution states that "To be elected as president of the Republic it is required to be Venezuelan by birth, to not hold another nationality, to be over thirty years old, to not be a member of the clergy, to not be subject to a court conviction and to meet the other requirements in this Constitution."[^cite_note-TSJ_contradice_a_Maduro_y_resuelve_el_misterio_de_su_nacionalidad-2016-245]

By 2014, official declarations by the Venezuela government shared four different birthplaces of Maduro.[^cite_note-246] [Tachira state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A1chira)'s governor [José Vielma Mora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Vielma_Mora) assured that Maduro was born in El Palotal sector of [San Antonio del Táchira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Antonio_del_T%C3%A1chira) and had relatives in the towns of Capacho and [Rubio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubio,_Venezuela).[^cite_note-247] The opposition deputy Abelardo Díaz reviewed the civil registry of El Valle, as well as the civil registry referenced by Vielma Mora, without finding any proof or documentation that could confirm Maduro's birthplace.[^cite_note-248] In June 2013, two months after assuming the presidency, Maduro claimed in a press conference in Rome that he was born in [Caracas](55289.md), in Los Chaguaramos, in [San Pedro Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Pedro_Parish). During an interview with a Spanish journalist, also in June 2013, [Elías Jaua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El%C3%ADas_Jaua) claimed that Maduro was born in El Valle parish, in the [Libertador Municipality of Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertador_Bolivarian_Municipality).[^cite_note-TSJ_contradice_a_Maduro_y_resuelve_el_misterio_de_su_nacionalidad-2016-245]

In October 2013, [Tibisay Lucena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibisay_Lucena), head of the National Electoral Council, assured in the [Globovisión](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globovisi%C3%B3n) TV show *[Vladimir a la 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Villegas)* that Maduro was born in [La Candelaria Parish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Candelaria_Parish) in Caracas, showing copies of the registry presentation book of all the newborns the day when allegedly Maduro was born. In April 2016 during a *[cadena nacional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_nacional)*, Maduro changed his birthplace narrative once more, saying that he was born in Los Chaguaramos, specifically in Valle Abajo, adding that he was baptized in the San Pedro church.[^cite_note-TSJ_contradice_a_Maduro_y_resuelve_el_misterio_de_su_nacionalidad-2016-245][^cite_note-249]

In 2016, a group of Venezuelans asked the National Assembly to investigate whether Maduro was Colombian in an [open letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_letter) addressed to the National Assembly president [Henry Ramos Allup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Ramos_Allup) that justified the request by the "reasonable doubts there are around the true origins of Maduro, because, to date, he has refused to show his birth certificate". The 62 petitioners, including former ambassador [Diego Arria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diego_Arria), businessman [Marcel Granier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Granier) and opposition former military, assuring that according to the Colombian constitution Maduro is "Colombian by birth" for being "the son of a Colombian mother and for having resided" in the neighboring country "during his childhood".[^cite_note-250]

The same year several former members of the Electoral Council sent an open letter to Tibisay Lucena requesting to "exhibit publicly, in a printed media of national circulation the documents that certify the strict compliance with Articles 41 and 227 of the Constitution of the Bolivarian Republic of Venezuela, that is to say, the birth certificate and the Certificate of Venezuelan Nationality by Birth of Nicolás Maduro Moros in order to verify if he is Venezuelan by birth and without another nationality". The document mentions that the current president of the CNE incurs in "a serious error, and even an irresponsibility, when she affirms that Maduro's nationality 'is not a motto of the National Electoral Council'" and the signatories also refer to the four different moments in which different politicians have awarded four different places of birth as official.[^cite_note-Diario_Las_Américas-2016-251] *[Diario Las Américas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diario_Las_Am%C3%A9ricas)* claimed to have access to the birth inscriptions of Teresa de Jesús Moros, Maduro's mother, and of José Mario Moros, his uncle, both registered in the parish church of San Antonio of [Cúcuta](623811.md), Colombia.[^cite_note-Diario_Las_Américas-2016-251]

Opposition deputies have assured that the birth certificate of Maduro must say that he is the son of a Colombian mother, which would represent proof that confirms that the president has double nationality and that he cannot hold any office under Article 41 of the constitution.[^cite_note-TSJ_contradice_a_Maduro_y_resuelve_el_misterio_de_su_nacionalidad-2016-245] Deputy [Dennis Fernández Solórzano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Fern%C3%A1ndez_Sol%C3%B3rzano?action=edit\&redlink=1) has headed a special commission that investigates the origins of the president and has declared that "Maduro's mother is a Colombian citizen" and that the Venezuelan head of State would also be Colombian.[^cite_note-Peñaloza-2016-252] The researcher, historian and former deputy Walter Márquez declared months after the presidential elections that Maduro's mother was born in Colombia and not in Rubio, Táchira. Márquez has also declared that Maduro "was born in Bogotá, according to the verbal testimonies of people who knew him as a child in Colombia and the documentary research we did" and that "there are more than 10 witnesses that corroborate this information, five of them live in Bogotá".[^cite_note-253]



Sentence of the [Supreme Tribunal in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile) that annuls the 2013 presidential elections and requests the presidency and the CNE to send a certified copy of the president's birth certificate, as well as the resignation from his Colombian nationality

On 28 October 2016, the Supreme Tribunal of Justice issued a ruling stating that according to incontrovertible evidence, it had absolute certainty that Maduro was born in Caracas, in the parish of La Candelaria, known then as the Libertador Department of the Federal District, on 23 November 1962.[^cite_note-TSJ_contradice_a_Maduro_y_resuelve_el_misterio_de_su_nacionalidad-2016-245] The ruling does not reproduce Maduro's birth certificate. Instead, it quotes a communique signed on 8 June by the Colombian vice-minister of foreign affairs Patti Londoño Jaramillo: "No related information was found, nor civil registry of birth, nor citizenship card that allows \[us] to infer that president Nicolás Maduro Moros is a Colombian national". The Supreme Court warned the deputies and the Venezuelans that "sowing doubts about the origins of the president" may "lead to the corresponding criminal, civil, administrative and, if applicable, disciplinary consequences" for "attack against the State".[^cite_note-Peñaloza-2016-252]

On 11 January 2018, the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile) decreed the nullity of the 2013 presidential elections after lawyer Enrique Aristeguieta Gramcko presented evidence about the presumed non-existence of ineligibility conditions of Maduro to be elected and to hold the office of the presidency. Aristeguieta argued in the appeal that, under Article 96, Section B, of the Political Constitution of Colombia, Nicolás Maduro Moros, even in the unproven case of having been born in Venezuela, is "Colombian by birth" because he is the son of a Colombian mother and by having resided in that territory during his youth. The Constitutional Chamber admitted the demand and requested the presidency and the Electoral Council to send a certified copy of the president's birth certificate, in addition to his resignation from Colombian nationality.[^cite_note-254] In March 2018 former Colombian president [Andrés Pastrana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Pastrana) made reference to the baptism certificate of Maduro's mother, noting that the disclosed document reiterates the Colombian origin of the mother of the president and that therefore Maduro has Colombian citizenship.[^cite_note-Peñaloza-2016-252]

### Conspiracy theories

Maduro continued the practice of his predecessor, Hugo Chávez, of denouncing alleged conspiracies against him or his government; in a period of fifteen months following his election, dozens of conspiracies, some supposedly linked to assassination and coup attempts, were reported by Maduro's government.[^cite_note-APconspiracy-255] In this same period, the number of attempted coups claimed by the Venezuelan government outnumbered all attempted and executed coups occurring worldwide in the same period.[^cite_note-FPcoupFATIGUE-256] In TV program *[La Hojilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Hojilla)*, [Mario Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Hojilla), a TV personality of the main state-run channel [Venezolana de Televisión](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezolana_de_Televisi%C3%B3n), stated in March 2015 that Maduro had received about 13 million psychological attacks.[^cite_note-257]

Observers say that Maduro uses such conspiracy theories as a strategy to distract Venezuelans from the root causes of problems facing his government.[^cite_note-APconspiracy-255][^cite_note-LAconspiracy-258][^cite_note-259][^cite_note-REUTERSjan2013-260] According to American news publication *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)*, Maduro's predecessor, Hugo Chávez, "relied on his considerable populist charm, conspiratorial rhetoric, and his prodigious talent for crafting excuses" to avoid backlash from troubles Venezuela was facing, with *Foreign Policy* further stating that for Maduro, "the appeal of reworking the magic that once saved his mentor is obvious".[^cite_note-FPcoupFATIGUE-256] Andrés Cañizales, a researcher at the [Andrés Bello Catholic University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Bello_Catholic_University), said that as a result of the lack of reliable mainstream [news broadcasting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_broadcasting), most Venezuelans stay informed via [social networking services](2041117.md), and [fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news) and [internet hoaxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_hoax) have a higher impact in Venezuela than in other countries.[^cite_note-261]

In early 2015, the Maduro government accused the United States of attempting to overthrow him. The Venezuelan government performed elaborate actions to respond to such alleged attempts and to convince the public that its claims were true.[^cite_note-FPcoupFATIGUE-256] The reactions included the arrest of [Antonio Ledezma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Ledezma) in February 2015, placing travel restrictions on American tourists and holding military marches and public exercises "for the first time in Venezuela's democratic history".[^cite_note-FPcoupFATIGUE-256] After the United States ordered sanctions to be placed on seven Venezuelan officials for human rights violations, Maduro used anti-U.S. rhetoric to bump up his approval ratings.[^cite_note-IBTapr15-262][^cite_note-SBS15-263] However, according to Venezuelan political scientist Isabella Picón, only about 15% of Venezuelans believed in the alleged coup attempt accusations at the time.[^cite_note-FPcoupFATIGUE-256]

In 2016, Maduro again claimed that the United States was attempting to assist the opposition with a coup attempt. On 12 January 2016, the Secretary General of the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS), [Luis Almagro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Almagro), threatened to invoke the [Inter-American Democratic Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Democratic_Charter), an instrument used to defend democracy in the Americas when threatened, when opposition National Assembly members were barred from taking their seats by the Maduro-aligned Supreme Court.[^cite_note-264] Human rights organizations such as [Human Rights Watch](46530.md),[^cite_note-HRWdc-265] and the [Human Rights Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Foundation)[^cite_note-266] called for the OAS to invoke the Democratic Charter. After more controversies and pursuing a recall on Maduro, on 2 May 2016, opposition members of the National Assembly met with OAS officials to ask for the body to implement the Democratic Charter.[^cite_note-MHmay16-267] Two days later on 4 May, the Maduro government called for a meeting the next day with the OAS, with Venezuelan Foreign Minister [Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md) stating that the United States and the OAS were attempting to overthrow Maduro.[^cite_note-KCS-268] On 17 May 2016 in a national speech, Maduro called OAS Secretary General Luis Almagro "a traitor" and stated that he worked for the CIA.[^cite_note-269] Almagro sent a letter rebuking Maduro, and refuting the claim.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

The Trump administration described Maduro's government as a "dictatorship".[^cite_note-272] When meeting with Latin American leaders during the [seventy-second session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seventy-second_session_of_the_United_Nations_General_Assembly) of the UN General Assembly, US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) discussed possible United States military intervention in Venezuela, an offer rejected by those present.[^cite_note-273] Maduro's son, [Nicolás Maduro Guerra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_Guerra), stated during the 5th [Constituent Assembly of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Constituent_Assembly_of_Venezuela) session that if the United States were to attack Venezuela, "the rifles would arrive in New York, Mr. Trump, we would arrive and take the [White House](33057.md)".[^cite_note-274]

[Michael Shifter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Shifter), president of the [Inter-American Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Dialogue) [think tank](37101.md), said "a military action of the United States against Venezuela would be contrary to the movements of the Trump administration to retire troops from [Syria](7515849.md) or [Afghanistan](737.md)."[^cite_note-275] [John Bolton](997107.md) declared that "all options are on the table" but has also said that "our objective is a peaceful transfer of power".[^cite_note-276]

### Censorship

Main articles: [Censorship in Venezuela](19533311.md) and [Censorship and media control during the Venezuelan presidential crisis](60360839.md)

During Maduro's first tenure, between 2013 and 2018, 115 media outlets were shut down, including 41 print outlets, 65 radio outlets and 9 television channels.[^cite_note-277] During the first seven months of 2019, the Press and Society Institute of Venezuela found at least 350 cases of violations of freedom of expression.[^cite_note-278]

Since the beginning of the [presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis), Venezuela has been exposed to frequent "information blackouts", periods without access to the Internet or other news services during important political events.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279][^cite_note-280] Since January, the National Assembly, and Guaido's speeches are regularly disrupted, television channels and radio programs have been censored, and many journalists were illegally detained.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279] The Venezuelan press workers union reported that in 2019, 40 journalists had been arrested unlawfully as of 12 March.[^cite_note-Escalona-281] As of June 2019, journalists have been denied access to seven sessions of the National Assembly by the National Guard.[^cite_note-282]

The state controls most Venezuelan television channels, and information unfavorable to the government is not covered completely.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279] Newspapers and magazines are scarce, as most cannot afford paper to print.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279] The underfunded web infrastructure has led to slow Internet connection speeds.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279] The information blackouts have promoted the creation of underground news coverage that is usually broadcast through social media and instant message services like [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp).[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279] The dependence of Venezuelans on social media has also promoted the spread of disinformation and pro-Maduro propaganda.[^cite_note-Nugent-2019-279]

Venezuela's rank on the [World Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) of [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) has dropped 42 places since 2013.[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] The [Inter-American Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Commission_on_Human_Rights) (IACHR) has made a call to the Maduro administration to reestablish TV and radio channels that have been closed, cease the restrictions on Internet access, and protect the rights of journalists.[^cite_note-OASmay-285][^cite_note-OAStalcual-286]

### Human rights

Further information: [Human rights in Venezuela](23438021.md) and [International Criminal Court investigation in Venezuela](62526848.md)

> "Let's overcome the differences, the conflict we had."
>
> "The doors of the Miraflores palace are open...so that we can talk about the differences we have, the conflict that arose and overcome it,"

— Maduro said on 23 April 2024 in an event held at the Miraflores presidential palace, with Karim Khan (the prosecutor of the International Criminal Court), who is investigating Venezuela for possible human right crimes, standing next to him. In the event Maduro said he agreed to allow the reopening of the office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights (OHCHR). The UN Human Rights Office was expelled from Venezuela in February after it had expressed concern over the detention of [Rocío San Miguel](61099564.md).

[^cite_note-MercoPress_2024_r972-287]

[^cite_note-288]

A Board of Independent Experts designated by the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS) published a 400-page report in 2018 that alleged that [crimes against humanity](61099.md) have been committed in Venezuela during Maduro's presidency.[^cite_note-289] The Board concluded that Maduro could be "responsible for dozens of murders, thousands of [extra-judicial executions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extra-judicial_executions), more than 12,000 cases of [arbitrary detentions](13435705.md), more than 290 cases of torture, attacks against the judiciary and a 'state-sanctioned humanitarian crisis' affecting hundreds of thousands of people".[^cite_note-LesaHum-290]

In February 2018, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) announced that it would open preliminary probes into the alleged [crimes against humanity](61099.md) performed by Venezuelan authorities.[^cite_note-291] On 27 September 2018, six states parties to the [Rome Statute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome_Statute_of_the_International_Criminal_Court): [Argentina](18951905.md), Canada, [Colombia](5222.md), Chile, [Paraguay](23420.md) and [Peru](170691.md), referred the situation in Venezuela since 12 February 2014 to the ICC, requesting the Prosecutor [Fatou Bensouda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatou_Bensouda) to initiate an investigation on crimes against humanity allegedly committed in the territory. The following day, the Presidency assigned the situation to Pre-Trial Chamber I.[^cite_note-292]

In March 2019 *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported in an article entitled "Maduro loses grip on Venezuela's poor, a vital source of his power" that barrios are turning against Maduro and that "many blame government brutality for the shift".[^cite_note-LosesGrip-293] [Foro Penal](54003280.md) said that 50 people—mostly in barrios—had been killed by security forces in only the first two months of the year, and 653 had been arrested for protesting or speaking against the government. Cofavic, a victims' rights group, estimated "3,717 extrajudicial killings in the past two years, mostly of suspected criminals in barrios".[^cite_note-LosesGrip-293]

In April 2019, the US Department of State alleged that Venezuela, "led by Nicolas Maduro, has consistently violated the human rights and dignity of its citizens" and "driven a once prosperous nation into economic ruin with his authoritarian rule" and that "Maduro's thugs have engaged in extra-judicial killings and torture, taken political prisoners, and severely restricted freedom of speech, all in a brutal effort to retain power."[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205] The State Department report highlighted abuse by the nation's security forces, including a number of deaths, the suspicious death of opposition politician [Fernando Albán Salazar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Alb%C3%A1n_Salazar), the detention of [Roberto Marrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Marrero), and repression of demonstrators during [Venezuelan protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_protests_\(2014%E2%80%93present\)) which left at least 40 dead in 2019.[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205]
