The third and last report of the [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](22463.md) addressed extrajudicial executions, torture, enforced disappearances, and other rights violations allegedly committed by Venezuelan security forces in recent years.[^cite_note-reutersIndependenceday-294] The High Commissioner [Michelle Bachelet](986814.md) expressed her concerns for the "shockingly high" number of extrajudicial killings and urged for the dissolution of the FAES.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-295] According to the report, 1569 cases of executions as consequence as a result of "resistance to authority" were registered by the Venezuelan authorities from 1 January to 19 March.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-295] Other 52 deaths that occurred during 2019 protests have been attributed to colectivos.[^cite_note-296] The report also details how the Venezuelan government has "aimed at neutralising, repressing and criminalising political opponents and people critical of the government" since 2016.[^cite_note-Fr24Michellereport-295]

A report by the human rights advocacy group [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) reported in September 2019 that the poor communities in [Venezuela](32374.md) no longer in support of Maduro's government have witnessed arbitrary arrests and extrajudicial executions at the hands of [Venezuelan police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_National_Police) unit. The Venezuelan government has repeatedly declared that the victims were armed criminals who had died during "confrontations". Still, several witnesses or families of victims have challenged these claims, and in many cases, victims were last seen alive in police custody. Although Venezuelan authorities told the [United Nations Office of the High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_of_the_High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights) (OHCHR) that five FAES agents were convicted on charges including attempted murder for crimes committed in 2018 and that 388 agents were under investigation for crimes committed between 2017 and 2019, the OHCHR also reported that "\[i]nstitutions responsible for the protection of human rights, such as the Attorney General's Office, the courts and the Ombudsperson, usually do not conduct prompt, effective, thorough, independent, impartial and transparent investigations into human rights violations and other crimes committed by State actors, bring perpetrators to justice, and protect victims and witnesses."[^cite_note-297] The government made three times more observations than the number of recommendations included in the UN report, and at the same time included false or incomplete claims.[^cite_note-298]

On 14 December 2020, the Office of the Prosecutor released a report on the office's year activities, stating that it believed there was a "reasonable basis" to think that "since at least April 2017, civilian authorities, members of the armed forces and pro-government individuals have committed the crimes against humanity." and that it expected to decide in 2021 whether to open an investigation or not.[^cite_note-Reuters-299] On 4 November 2021, ICC Prosecutor [Karim Khan](11606957.md) announced the opening of an investigation regarding the situation in Venezuela.[^cite_note-300]

The Venezuelan human rights organization [PROVEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PROVEA) has identified more than 43,000 people whose "right to personal integrity" has been violated since Maduro took office in 2013, including 1,652 people who were tortured, 7,309 people who were subjected to "cruel, inhumane and degrading" treatment or punishment, and at least 28 people who were killed in the country's prisons.[^cite_note-301]

### Judicial independence

On 16 September 2021, the Independent International Fact-Finding Mission on Venezuela released its second report on the country's situation, concluding that the independence of the Venezuelan justice system under Maduro has been deeply eroded to the extent of playing an important role in aiding the state repression and perpetuating state impunity for human rights violations. The document identified frequent [due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process) violations, including the use of pre-trial detention as a routine (rather than an exceptional measure) and judges sustaining detentions or charges based on manipulated or fabricated evidence, evidence obtained through illegal means, and evidence obtained through coercion or torture; in some of the reviewed cases, the judges also failed to protect torture victims, returning them to detentions centers were torture was denounced, "despite having heard victims, sometimes bearing visible injuries consistent with torture, make the allegation in court". The report also concluded that prosecutorial and judicial individuals at all levels witnessed or experienced external interference in decision-making and that several reported receiving instructions either from the judicial or prosecutorial hierarchy or from political officials on how to decide cases.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304]

### Alleged drug trafficking and money laundering

Main articles: [Narcosobrinos incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narcosobrinos_incident), [Cartel of the Suns](45272133.md), [Tren de Aragua](70859925.md), and [Tareck El Aissami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tareck_El_Aissami)

See also: [Manuel Noriega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Noriega)

#### Narcosobrinos incident

Two nephews of Maduro's wife, Efraín Antonio Campo Flores and Francisco Flores de Freitas, were found guilty in a US court of conspiracy to import cocaine in November 2016, with some of their funds possibly assisting Maduro's presidential campaign in the [2013 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election) and potentially for the [2015 Venezuelan parliamentary elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Venezuelan_parliamentary_election), with the funds mainly used to "help their family stay in power".[^cite_note-INSIGHT-305][^cite_note-Blasco-2015-306][^cite_note-REUT18nov-307] One informant stated that the two often flew out of Terminal 4 of [Simon Bolivar Airport](8277069.md), a terminal reserved for the president.[^cite_note-INSIGHT-305][^cite_note-Blasco-2015-306]

After Maduro's nephews were apprehended by the US [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md) for the illegal distribution of cocaine on 10 November 2015, carrying diplomatic passports, Maduro posted a statement on Twitter criticizing "attacks and imperialist ambushes", saying "the Father land will continue on its path".[^cite_note-308] [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello), a senior official in Maduro's government, was quoted as saying the arrests were a "kidnapping" by the United States.[^cite_note-309]

On 18 November 2016, the two nephews were found guilty.[^cite_note-REUT18nov-307] On 14 December 2017, the two [were sentenced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_of_America_v._Campo_and_Flores) to 18 years of imprisonment.[^cite_note-310]

In October 2022, Maduro's nephews were freed in a prisoner swap for seven American directors of the oil refinery corporation [CITGO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CITGO) (part of the [Citgo Six](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citgo_Six)) who were imprisoned in Venezuela.[^cite_note-311]

#### Sanctions against Tareck El Aissami and Diosdado Cabello

Ex-Venezuelan vice president [Tareck El Aissami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tareck_El_Aissami), who served Maduro from 2017 to 2018, is under U.S. sanctions for drug trafficking and aiding [state terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_terrorism). He is accused by the [U.S State Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S_State_Department) of aiding sanctioned Iran-backed terrorist organizations, including [Hezbollah](13919.md) and [Quds Force](4028355.md). He is also accused of having ties with various illicit organizations, including [Los Zetas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Zetas) and [Cartel of the Suns](45272133.md).[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313]

On 18 May 2018, the [Office of Foreign Assets Control](231045.md) (OFAC) of the [United States Department of the Treasury](53667.md) placed sanctions in effect against high-level official [Diosdado Cabello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diosdado_Cabello). OFAC stated that Cabello and others used their power within the Bolivarian government "to personally profit from extortion, money laundering, and embezzlement", with Cabello allegedly directing drug trafficking activities with Venezuelan Vice President [Tareck El Aissami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tareck_El_Aissami) while dividing drug profits with President Maduro.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

#### US indictment of Maduro

On 26 March 2020, the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md) charged Maduro and other Venezuelan officials and some Colombian former FARC members for what Attorney General [William Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Barr) described as "narco-terrorism": the shipping of [cocaine](7701.md) to the US to wage a health war on US citizens. According to Barr, Venezuelan leaders and the FARC faction organised an "air bridge" from a Venezuelan air base transporting cocaine to Central America and a sea route to the Caribbean. The US government offered \$15 million for any information that would lead to his arrest.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-nyt2020-317][^cite_note-npr2020-318][^cite_note-319] Nicolas Maduro has frequently denied any ties to a drug trafficking syndicate.[^cite_note-320]

##### Reward increases

After Maduro was inaugurated for a [third term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_inauguration_of_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro) on 10 January 2025, the US State Department announced that the reward against Maduro was increased from \$15 million to \$25 million. National Security Council spokesperson [John Kirby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kirby_\(admiral\)) said that the decision to raise the bounty as part of "a concerted message of solidarity with the Venezuelan people", meant "to further elevate international efforts to maintain pressure on Maduro and his representatives".[^cite_note-321] Secretary of State [Antony Blinken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antony_Blinken) said that the US "does not recognize Nicolas Maduro as the president of Venezuela" and a US Treasury Under Secretary, Bradley Smith, added that the US stood with its "likeminded partners" in "solidarity with the people's vote for new leadership and rejects Maduro's fraudulent claim of victory".[^cite_note-322]

US Attorney General [Pam Bondi](4550623.md)'s August 2025 announcement that the reward for Maduro's arrest has increased to \$50 million.

The reward was increased again to \$50 million on 7 August 2025, with Attorney General [Pam Bondi](4550623.md) accusing Maduro of collaborating with foreign terrorist organizations, such as [Tren de Aragua](70859925.md), the [Sinaloa Cartel](16881931.md), and the [Cartel of the Suns](45272133.md), to bring deadly violence to the United States. In a video message, Bondi described Maduro as one of the "world's most notorious narco-traffickers" and a "threat to national security", which prompted the reward to be doubled. She concluded, "Under President Trump's leadership, Maduro will not escape justice, and he will be held accountable for his despicable crimes", before providing the public with a hotline number to report tips to the [Drug Enforcement Administration](146720.md).[^cite_note-323]

In a [Fox News](11121.md) interview, Bondi stated that the DOJ had seized approximately \$700 million in assets linked to Maduro. The assets allegedly included multiple luxury homes in [Florida](18933066.md), a mansion in the [Dominican Republic](8060.md), private jets, vehicles, a horse farm, jewelry, and large sums of cash.[^cite_note-324] Bondi described Maduro's government as an "organized crime operation" that continued to function despite the seizures.[^cite_note-325]

This led to mockery of Bondi and the Trump administration on social media, with the phrase "He's in Venezuela" trending on [Twitter](9988187.md). Some internet users claimed it was a deliberate distraction from the controversy surrounding the [Jeffrey Epstein client list](80415168.md).[^cite_note-326] Venezuelan Foreign Minister [Yván Gil](76565361.md) dismissed the announcement as a "crude political propaganda operation", adding that he was not surprised given Bondi's failure to deliver the client list of the convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328]

Venezuelan Vice President [Delcy Rodríguez](44199228.md) dismissed Bondi's allegations as a "shameless", accusing her of staging a "cardboard cutout" performance to continue a "ridiculous and cheap show" against Maduro. Rodríguez further claimed that Bondi was not in her right mind, suggesting that the Epstein case was keeping her up at night.[^cite_note-329] Maduro, for his part, dared Trump to arrest him during a [nationally televised speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadena_nacional) and warned American leaders not to even attempt such an action, saying it would provoke a response that could also lead to the end of the American empire.[^cite_note-330][^cite_note-331]

### Homophobic statements

As foreign minister, during a tenth anniversary gathering commemorating the [2002 Venezuelan coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Venezuelan_coup_d'%C3%A9tat_attempt) going into the [2012 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Venezuelan_presidential_election), Maduro called opposition members "snobs" and "little faggots".[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]

During the presidential campaign of 2013, Maduro used anti-gay attacks as a political weapon, calling representatives of the opposition "faggots".[^cite_note-homofobia-334] Maduro used anti-gay speech toward his opponent [Henrique Capriles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles) calling him a "little princess".[^cite_note-homofobia-334][^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336]

In 2017, Maduro expressed his personal support for same-sex marriage.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

### Hunger crisis

In August 2017, [Luisa Ortega Díaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luisa_Ortega_D%C3%ADaz), Chief Prosecutor of Venezuela from 2007 until her sacking in August 2017, accused Maduro of profiting from the [shortages in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_in_Venezuela). The government-operated [Local Committees for Supply and Production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Committees_for_Supply_and_Production) (CLAP), which provides food to impoverished Venezuelans, made contracts with Group Grand Limited, a company that Ortega said was "presumably owned by Nicolás Maduro" through front-men Rodolfo Reyes, Álvaro Uguedo Vargas and Alex Saab. The Venezuelan government paid Group Grand Limited, a Mexican entity, for basic foods, which it supplied to CLAP. Maduro accused Ortega of working with the United States to damage his government.[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

An April 2019 communication from the [United States Department of State](31975.md) highlighted a 2017 [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)) investigation finding that the government paid US\$42 for food that cost under US\$13 and that "Maduro's inner circle kept the difference, which totaled more than \$200 million dollars in at least one case", adding that food boxes were "distributed in exchange for votes".[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205] On 18 October 2018, Mexican prosecutors accused the Venezuelan government and Mexican individuals of buying poor-quality food products for CLAP and exporting them to Venezuela to double their value for sale.[^cite_note-342]

During the [Venezuelan presidential crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuelan_presidential_crisis), Venezuelan National Assembly president [Juan Guaidó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Guaid%C3%B3) said that the Maduro government had plans to steal for humanitarian purposes the products that entered the country, including plans to distribute these products through the government's food-distribution program CLAP.[^cite_note-343]

While Venezuelans were affected by hunger and shortages, Maduro and his government officials publicly shared images of themselves eating luxurious meals, images that were met with displeasure by Venezuelans.[^cite_note-El_Cooperate-2018-344] Despite the majority of Venezuelans losing weight due to hunger, members of the Maduro's administration appeared to gain weight.[^cite_note-El_Cooperate-2018-344]

In November 2017, while giving a lengthy, live *cadena* broadcast, Maduro, unaware he was still being filmed, pulled out an [empanada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empanada) from his desk and began eating it.[^cite_note-Pastrygate-345] This occurred amid controversy over Maduro's gaining weight during the nationwide food and medicine shortage; with many on social media criticizing the publicly broadcast incident.[^cite_note-Pastrygate-345][^cite_note-346]

In September 2018, Maduro ate at one of [Salt Bae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_Bae)'s luxury restaurants in Istanbul, where he and his wife were served a meat-heavy meal and offered a personalized shirt and box of cigars with Maduro's name engraved.[^cite_note-El_Cooperate-2018-344][^cite_note-347] *The Wall Street Journal* reported that the incident received international criticism and left poor Venezuelans incensed.[^cite_note-LosesGrip-293]

In December 2018, videos and pictures were leaked showing a glamorous Christmas party that included an expensive feast, including French wine, taking place in the seat of the pro-Maduro [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)). The images received considerable backlash from social networks, criticizing the costs of the party during the grave economic crisis in the country and the hypocrisy of Maduro's government.[^cite_note-348]

### Corruption

Further information: [Corruption in Venezuela](23453241.md) and [Operation Car Wash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Car_Wash)



The *"Corrupt Venezuelan Regime"*, according to the [United States Department of Justice](52563.md)

In an investigative interview with Euzenando Prazeres de Azevedo, president of Constructora Odebrecht in Venezuela, the executive revealed how Odebrecht paid \$35 million to fund Maduro's [2013 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election) if Odebrecht projects would be prioritized in Venezuela.[^cite_note-WPode-349] Americo Mata, Maduro's campaign manager, initially asked for \$50 million for Maduro, though the final \$35 million was settled.[^cite_note-WPode-349][^cite_note-350]

Maduro was sentenced to 18 years and 3 months in prison on 15 August 2018 by the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice of Venezuela in exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_of_Venezuela_in_exile), with the exiled high court stating "there is enough evidence to establish the guilt ... \[of] corruption and legitimation of capital".[^cite_note-351] The [Organization of American States](22788.md) Secretary General, Luis Almagro, supported the verdict and asked for the Venezuelan National Assembly to recognize the ruling of the Supreme Tribunal in exile.[^cite_note-352]

The US State Department issued a fact sheet stating that Maduro's most serious corruption involved embezzlement in which "a European bank accepted exorbitant commissions to process approximately \$2 billion in transactions related to Venezuelan third–party money launderers, shell companies, and complex financial products to siphon off funds from PdVSA".[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205] The State Department also alleges that Maduro expelled authorized foreign companies from the mining sector to allow officials to exploit Venezuela's resources for their own gain, using unregulated miners under the control of Venezuela's armed forces.[^cite_note-MaduroViola-205]

### Sanctions

Main article: [International sanctions during the Venezuelan crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_Venezuelan_crisis)

Announcement of sanctions against Maduro by National Security Advisor [H. R. McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._R._McMaster) and Secretary of the Treasury [Steven Mnuchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Mnuchin)

Thirteen government officials were sanctioned by the [United States Department of Treasury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Treasury) due to their involvement with the [2017 Venezuelan Constituent Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Venezuelan_Constituent_Assembly_election).[^cite_note-TREASven-353] Two months later, the Canadian government sanctioned members of the Maduro government, including Maduro, preventing Canadian nationals from participating in property and financial deals with him due to the rupture of Venezuela's constitutional order.[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]

After the Constituent Assembly election, the United States sanctioned Maduro on 31 July 2017, making him the fourth foreign [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state) to be sanctioned by the United States after Bashar al-Assad of Syria, [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md) of North Korea and [Robert Mugabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mugabe) of Zimbabwe.[^cite_note-Herald2017-356] Secretary of the Treasury [Steven Mnuchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Mnuchin) stating "Maduro is a dictator who disregards the will of the Venezuelan people".[^cite_note-SANCTIONED-105] Maduro fired back at the sanctions during his victory speech saying "I don't obey imperial orders. I'm against the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) that governs the [White House](33057.md), and I'm proud to feel that way."[^cite_note-Herald2017-356]

In March 2018, Maduro was sanctioned by the Panamanian government for his alleged involvement with "money laundering, financing of terrorism and financing the proliferation of [weapons of mass destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_of_mass_destruction)".[^cite_note-PanamaSanc-357]

Maduro is also banned from entering [Colombia](5222.md).[^cite_note-Colombia31Jan2019-358] The Colombian government maintains a list of people banned from entering Colombia or subject to expulsion; as of January 2019, the list had 200 people with a "close relationship and support for the Nicolás Maduro regime".[^cite_note-MaduroHeads-359][^cite_note-Colombia31Jan2019-358]

After the breakdown of the Barbados Agreements, he disqualified the pre-candidate [Maria Corina Machado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maria_Corina_Machado) through the Supreme Court, similar to the official government. After that, the US sanctions were reapplied under the [Biden administration](65732174.md).[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362]

In September 2024, an Argentine federal court issued an arrest warrant against Maduro and several other Venezuelan officials for crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-363]

In January 2026, the Swiss government imposed a four-year freeze on any assets held by Maduro and his close associates in Switzerland.[^cite_note-364] That same month, Florida governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) said that the [Attorney General of Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_Florida) was “looking very seriously” at the possibility of filing state charges against Maduro, accusing him of bringing drugs to [Florida](18933066.md).[^cite_note-365]

## Public opinion

See also: [List of heads of the executive by approval rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_the_executive_by_approval_rating)



**Source:** Data Analysis to July 2017. In March 2019, Maduro's approval rating was 14%.

[^cite_note-Wyss4Mar-366]

In October 2013, Maduro's approval rating stood between 45% and 50% with Reuters stating that it was possibly due to Hugo Chávez's endorsement.[^cite_note-367] One year later in October 2014, Maduro's approval rating was at 24.5% according to pollster Datanálisis.[^cite_note-368] In November 2014, Datanálisis polls indicated that more than 66% of Venezuelans believed that Maduro should not finish his six-year term, with government supporters representing more than 25% of those believing that Maduro should resign.[^cite_note-BInov2014-369] In March and April 2015, Maduro saw a small increase in approval after initiating a campaign of anti-US rhetoric following the sanctioning of seven officials accused by the United States of participating in human rights violations.[^cite_note-IBTapr15-262][^cite_note-SBS15-263]

During a recall movement gathered from late-October through November 2016, a poll by Venebarómetro found that "88% of 'likely' voters in a recall would choose to oust Maduro."[^cite_note-370] Among similar polls, Hercon concluded that up to 81.3% of voters responded being willing to recall Maduro,[^cite_note-371] Meganálisis that up to 78.3% of respondents disagreed that Maduro continued governing,[^cite_note-372] and Datanálisis that 75% of Venezuelans considered that Maduro should be recalled.[^cite_note-373]

In September 2018, Meganálisis polls found that 84.6% of Venezuelans surveyed wanted Maduro and his government to be removed from power.[^cite_note-374] Following the suspension of the recall movement, a Venebarómetro poll found that 61.4% found that Maduro had become a dictator,[^cite_note-375] while in a poll taken by Keller and Associates 63% of those questioned thought that Maduro was a dictator.[^cite_note-376]

Before the [2018 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election), a Datanálisis poll indicated that 16.7% of voters would vote for Maduro as candidate, compared to 27.6% and 17.1% of voters that would choose rival candidates [Henri Falcón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Falc%C3%B3n) and [Javier Bertucci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_Bertucci), respectively.[^cite_note-377]

During the presidential crisis, *The Wall Street Journal* reported that [*barrios*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barrio) were turning against Maduro in "a shift born of economic misery and police violence".[^cite_note-LosesGrip-293] Surveys between 30 January and 1 February 2019 by Meganálisis recorded that 4.1% of Venezuelans recognized Maduro as president, 11.2% were undecided, and 84.6% of respondents recognized Guaidó as interim president. The study of 1,030 Venezuelans was conducted in 16 states and 32 cities.[^cite_note-378]

A separate pollster, Hinterlaces, ran a poll from 21 January to 2 February 2019 that found that 57% of Venezuelans recognized Maduro as the legitimate president of Venezuela, 32% recognized Guaidó, and 11% were unsure.[^cite_note-379] By 4 March 2019, a Datanálisis poll found Guaidó's approval at 61%, and Maduro's at all-time low of 14%, with Guaidó win 77% in a theoretical election with Maduro, who received 23% of support.[^cite_note-Wyss4Mar-366] Datanálisis found that, among the poorest 20% of Venezuelans, Maduro's support had fallen to 18% in February 2019 from 40% two years earlier.[^cite_note-LosesGrip-293]

In a May 2019 analysis, José Briceño-Ruiz, based on the Meganalisis poll and other trends, said that Maduro was "extremely unpopular".[^cite_note-380]

In February 2023, a year before the [2024 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Venezuelan_presidential_election), a Datincorp poll concluded that 15.69% of voters would vote for Maduro as a candidate, in contrast to 16.86% that would vote for opposition candidate [María Corina Machado](5791389.md).[^cite_note-381]

## In popular culture

- Maduro was parodied in the [animated web series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_animation) *[Isla Presidencial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isla_Presidencial)*, along with most of the other Latin American leaders, portrayed as a man of limited intelligence, twisted speech, and capable of talking with birds, the latter being a reference to a comment made by Maduro during the [2013 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election), when he said that the late Chávez had reincarnated in a little bird and talked to him to bless his candidacy.

  [^cite_note-382]

- The [2020 revival of the *Animaniacs* series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animaniacs_\(2020_TV_series\)) has featured Maduro, mocking him and the [hyperinflation in Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperinflation_in_Venezuela).

  [^cite_note-383]

  [^cite_note-384]

- Several documentaries that discuss the Bolivarian Revolution and the Crisis in Venezuela, including *[In the Shadow of the Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_the_Shadow_of_the_Revolution),*

  [^cite_note-385]

  &#x20;*[Chavismo: The Plague of the 21st Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chavismo:_The_Plague_of_the_21st_Century),[^cite_note-386]* *[El pueblo soy yo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_pueblo_soy_yo),[^cite_note-387]* and *[A La Calle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_La_Calle),[^cite_note-388]* depict Maduro as well.

|  | Bicentenary Order of the Admirable Campaign | [Venezuela](32374.md) | 15 June 2013 | [Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trujillo_\(state\)), Venezuela | Venezuelan order.[^cite_note-393] |
|  | [Star of Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decorations,_medals_and_badges_of_Palestine) | [Palestine](241405.md) | 16 May 2014 | [Caracas](55289.md), Venezuela | Highest decoration of Palestine.[^cite_note-394] |
|  | Order of Augusto César Sandino | [Nicaragua](21362.md) | 17 March 2015 | [Managua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Managua), Nicaragua | Highest decoration of Nicaragua.[^cite_note-395] |
|  | [Order of José Martí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Jos%C3%A9_Mart%C3%AD) | [Cuba](5042481.md) | 18 March 2016 | [La Habana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Habana), Cuba | Cuban order.[^cite_note-396] |
|  | Order of Francisco Morazán | [Honduras](13394.md) | 28 January 2024 | [Tegucigalpa](25994100.md), Honduras | Highest decoration of Honduras.[^cite_note-397] |

### Others

- In 2014, Maduro was named as one of *TIME* magazine's [100 Most Influential People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_100). In the article, it explained that whether or not Venezuela collapses "now depends on Maduro", saying it also depends on whether Maduro "can step out of the shadow of his pugnacious predecessor and compromise with his opponents".

  [^cite_note-TIMEmaduro-398]

- In 2016, the [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md) (RSF) *Top 35 Predators of Press Freedom* list placed Maduro as a "predator" to press freedom in Venezuela, with RSF noting his method of "carefully orchestrated censorship and economic asphyxiation" toward media organizations.

  [^cite_note-399]

  [^cite_note-400]

- In 2016, the [Organized Crime and Corruption Reporting Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_Crime_and_Corruption_Reporting_Project) (OCCRP), an international non-governmental organization that investigates crime and corruption, gave President Maduro the *Person of the Year Award* that "recognizes the individual who has done the most in the world to advance organized criminal activity and corruption". The OCCRP stated that they "chose Maduro for the global award on the strength of his corrupt and oppressive reign, so rife with mismanagement that citizens of his oil-rich nation are literally starving and begging for medicines" and that Maduro and his family steal millions of dollars from government coffers to fund patronage that maintains President Maduro's power in Venezuela. The group also explains how Maduro had overruled the legislative branch filled with opposition politicians, repressed citizen protests and had relatives involved in drug trafficking.

  [^cite_note-NMoccrp-401]

## Elections

### 2013 presidential campaign

Main article: [2013 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Venezuelan_presidential_election)

Maduro won the second presidential election after the death of Hugo Chávez, with 50.61% of the votes against the opposition's candidate [Henrique Capriles Radonski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Capriles_Radonski) who had 49.12% of the votes.[^cite_note-402] The [Democratic Unity Roundtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Unity_Roundtable) contested his election as fraud and as a violation of the constitution. However, the [Supreme Court of Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Venezuela) ruled that under Venezuela's Constitution, Maduro is the legitimate president and was invested as such by the Venezuelan [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_\(Venezuela\)) (Asamblea Nacional).[^cite_note-403]

### 2018 presidential campaign

Main articles: [2018 Venezuelan presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election) and [2018 Nicolás Maduro presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro_presidential_campaign)



Nicolás Maduro's 2018 presidential campaign logo

Maduro was declared as the winner of the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Venezuelan_presidential_election) with 67.8% of the vote. The result was denounced as fraudulent by most neighboring countries, including Argentina, [Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto)'s Mexico, Chile, Colombia, Brazil, Canada and the United States,[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405] as well as organizations such as the [European Union](9317.md),[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407] and the [Organization of American States](22788.md), but recognized as legitimate by other neighboring countries such as [López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador)'s Mexico,[^cite_note-408] Bolivia,[^cite_note-409] Cuba,[^cite_note-410] [Suriname](26828.md),[^cite_note-411] Nicaragua[^cite_note-412] and some other [ALBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALBA) countries,[^cite_note-413][^cite_note-414] along with South Africa,[^cite_note-415] China,[^cite_note-416] Russia,[^cite_note-417] [North Korea](21255.md),[^cite_note-418] and [Turkey](11125639.md).[^cite_note-419]

### 2024 presidential campaign

Main article: [2024 Venezuelan presidential election](60825396.md)

Maduro ran for a third consecutive term, while González represented the [Unitary Platform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_Platform) (Spanish: Plataforma Unitaria Democrática; PUD), the main opposition political alliance. In June 2023, the Venezuelan government had barred leading candidate [María Corina Machado](5791389.md) from participating.[^cite_note-MCM-420][^cite_note-421] This move was regarded by the opposition as a violation of political human rights and was condemned by international bodies such as the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS),[^cite_note-OAS_report_VE_pres_elec_DECO-422] the [European Union](9317.md),[^cite_note-EUopposition-423] and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md),[^cite_note-424] as well as numerous countries.[^cite_note-425]

Academics, news outlets and the opposition provided strong evidence showing that González won the election by a wide margin[^cite_note-GuardianEvidenceShows-40][^cite_note-Kurmanaev-2024-38][^cite_note-426] with the opposition releasing copies of official [tally sheets](#Electoral_process) collected by [poll watchers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_watchers) from a majority of polling centers showing a landslide victory for González.[^cite_note-OAS_report_VE_pres_elec_DECO-422][^cite_note-opposition_results_website-427][^cite_note-LaPatilla_how2access_electoral_records-428] The government-controlled [National Electoral Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_\(Venezuela\)) (CNE) announced[^cite_note-429] [possibly falsified](382165.md)[^cite_note-ElEspectador_decimal_places-430][^cite_note-Infobae_burdo-431] results claiming a narrow Maduro victory on 29 July; vote tallies were not provided.[^cite_note-GuardianPressureGrows-432] The [Carter Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_Center) was unable to verify the CNE's results, asserting the election failed to meet international democratic election standards.[^cite_note-433]

The CNE's results were rejected by the OAS,[^cite_note-OAS_report_VE_pres_elec_DECO-422] and the [United Nations](31769.md) declared that there was "no precedent in contemporary democratic elections" for announcing a winner without providing tabulated results.[^cite_note-UNBlasts-434] Analyses by media sources found the CNE results statistically improbable and lacking in credibility.[^cite_note-APReview-435][^cite_note-WAPOMaduroLost-436][^cite_note-ElEspectador_decimal_places-430] [Parallel vote tabulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quick_count) confirmed the win by González.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-AtlanticDictatorLies-438][^cite_note-AltaVista_PVT_results-439] Political scientist [Steven Levitsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Levitsky) called the official results "one of the most egregious electoral frauds in modern Latin American history".[^cite_note-440]

[Protests occurred across the country and internationally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Venezuelan_protests), as the Maduro administration initiated [Operation Tun Tun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tun_Tun), a crackdown on dissent. [Some world leaders rejected the CNE's claimed results](77467581.md) and recognized González as the election winner,[^cite_note-BBC2Aug-39][^cite_note-HardtoBelieve-441][^cite_note-442] while some other countries, including Russia, China, Iran, North Korea and Cuba recognized Maduro as the winner.[^cite_note-WorldLeadersCast-443][^cite_note-TimesFlees-444] Maduro did not cede power,[^cite_note-GuardianRefusal-445] and instead asked the [Supreme Tribunal of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Tribunal_of_Justice_\(Venezuela\)) (TSJ), composed of justices loyal to Maduro,[^cite_note-GuardianPressureGrows-432][^cite_note-WPBidenDeal-446][^cite_note-LoyaltoMaduro-447][^cite_note-HandPicked-448] to audit and approve the results.[^cite_note-ReutersCourtRatifies-449][^cite_note-BBCStayinPower-450][^cite_note-CriesFoul-451][^cite_note-APCourtCertifies-452]

On 22 August, as anticipated,[^cite_note-BBCStayinPower-450][^cite_note-LoyaltoMaduro-447][^cite_note-HandPicked-448] the TSJ described the CNE's statement of Maduro winning the election as "validated".[^cite_note-ReutersCourtRatifies-449] The supreme court ruling was rejected by the United States, the European Union and ten Latin American countries.[^cite_note-453][^cite_note-454] An arrest warrant was issued on 2 September for González for the alleged crimes of "usurpation of functions, falsification of public documents, instigation to disobey the law, conspiracy and association", according to Reuters.[^cite_note-APArrestWarrant-455] After seeking asylum in the [Spanish Embassy in Caracas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_Spain,_Caracas), González left for Spain on 7 September.[^cite_note-456] Maduro was sworn in for a third term on 10 January 2025.[^cite_note-457]

Nicolás Maduro is expected to be re-elected president of Venezuela on May 20 in an election that most experts agree is a sham

[^cite_note-ShamFT-113]: &#x20;["Venezuela's sham presidential election"](https://www.ft.com/content/edd6c4a0-5906-11e8-b8b2-d6ceb45fa9d0). *[Financial Times](136566.md)*. 16 May 2018. The vote, of course, is a sham. Support is bought via ration cards issued to state workers with the implicit threat that both job and card are at risk if they vote against the government. Meanwhile, the country's highest profile opposition leaders are barred from running, in exile, or under arrest.

[Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20190115182319/https://www.reuters.com/article/us-venezuela-politics-idUSKCN0VL0AE) from the original on 15 January 2019. Retrieved 15 January 2019. Venezuela's Supreme Court approved President Nicolas Maduro's "economic emergency" decree on Thursday, setting up a showdown after the Venezuelan Congress rejected the measure last month. Maduro's decree includes wider executive powers to control the budget, companies and the currency amid a severe economic crisis in the OPEC nation.
